Proof of Theorem 3
By construction, Algorithm 1 terminates through exactly one of three
mutually exclusive routes: it crosses the upper boundary (event E+), crosses the lower boundary
(event E−), or reaches the end of the fixed pool without crossing either boundary and executes the
pre-committed fallback (event Efb); in particular E+,E−,Efb are pairwise disjoint.
Consequently the event that the policy rejects H0 is the disjoint union of the upper boundary
event E+ and the fallback-rejection event Efb ∩{fallback rejects} ⊆Efb, so by countable additivity
of probability over disjoint events,
Pπk
0 (δπk = 1) = Pπk
0 (E+) + Pπk
0
 Efb ∩{fallback rejects}

.
By Lemmas EC.5 and EC.6, the two terms on the right are at most α1,k and α2,k respectively, so
Pπk
0 (δπk = 1) ≤α1,k + α2,k = αk,
where the equality is the budget identity following (23).
Likewise, the event that the policy accepts H0 under H1 is the disjoint union of the lower
boundary event E−and the fallback-acceptance event Efb ∩{fallback accepts}, so
Pπk
1 (δπk = 0) = Pπk
1 (E−) + Pπk
1
 Efb ∩{fallback accepts}

.
The same two lemmas and (24) give
Pπk
1 (δπk = 0) ≤β1,k + β2,k = βk.
□

We advance the proof of Lemma EC.12 as the proofs of Lemmas EC.8-EC.11 rely on the conclusions

Lemmas EC.8-EC.11.
To prove Lemma EC.12, we need auxiliary Lemma EC.13. We relegate the proof of Lemma EC.13
to Appendix EC.3.2.1.

Proof of Lemma EC.13 In this subsection, we prove Lemma EC.13. We first
present an auxiliary Lemma EC.15, and then present the proof of Lemma EC.13 at the end of this
subsection.
To introduce Lemma EC.15, we motivate the quantity V (u,v) that will appear in Lemma EC.15.
Consider the family of threshold tests that reject H0 when
¯Xn := n−1 Pn
i=1 Xi ≥ϑ for some
ϑ ∈(p0,p1). For p ∈(0,1), let Pp denote the probability law under which X1,X2,... are i.i.d.
Bernoulli(p), and let Ep denote expectation with respect to Pp. By the Chernoff bound for Bernoulli
sums,
Pp0
  ¯Xn ≥ϑ

≤e−nD0(ϑ),
Pp1
  ¯Xn ≤ϑ

≤e−nD1(ϑ),
where D0(ϑ) := kl(ϑ∥p0) and D1(ϑ) := kl(ϑ∥p1). Let uk := log(1/αk), vk := log(1/βk). To satisfy
the type-I error target e−uk = αk, it suffices that nD0(ϑ) ≥uk, i.e., n ≥uk/D0(ϑ). Symmetrically,
the type-II error target e−vk = βk requires n ≥vk/D1(ϑ). For a fixed threshold ϑ, the smallest
sample size at which both Chernoff bounds simultaneously deliver the target errors is therefore
max

uk
D0(ϑ),
vk
D1(ϑ)

,
and optimizing over ϑ ∈(p0,p1) yields the quantity
V (u,v) :=
inf
ϑ∈(p0,p1)max

u
D0(ϑ),
v
D1(ϑ)

.
(EC.34)
This construction immediately gives the upper bound Nfixed,H(αk,βk) ≤V (uk,vk)+1 by exhibiting
an explicit feasible test. The next lemma asserts that this Chernoff-style bound is in fact first-
order tight: no test on i.i.d. Bernoulli labels can achieve the target errors with substantially fewer
samples. Recall that Lk := max{uk,vk}.
Lemma EC.15 (First-order sample-size identity for the full-label benchmark). Let
αk,βk ↓0 satisfy Assumption 3. Then V (uk,vk) = Θ(Lk) and
V (uk,vk) −O(log Lk) ≤Nfixed,H(e−uk,e−vk) ≤V (uk,vk) + 1.
(EC.35)
Proof of Lemma EC.15
The proof proceeds in five steps. Step 1 establishes the monotonicity
of D0 and D1 and shows that V (uk,vk) = Θ(Lk). Step 2 applies Chernoff bounds to an optimally
chosen sample-mean threshold test, yielding Nfixed,H ≤V (uk,vk) + 1. Step 3 derives a lower bound
on Bernoulli type-class probabilities Pp(Pn
i=1 Xi = j). Step 4 shows that, for any feasible test, no
type class can have probability exceeding the respective error levels under both hypotheses. Step 5
combines Steps 3–4 to show that a sample size below V (uk −δk,vk −δk), where δk = O(log Lk),
would violate this incompatibility. Assumption 3 then gives Nfixed,H ≥V (uk,vk) −O(log Lk).

Step 2: Chernoff upper bound. We prove the bound
Nfixed,H(e−uk,e−vk) ≤V (uk,vk) + 1.

For ϑ ∈(p0,p1), Chernoff’s bound for Bernoulli sums gives
Pp0
  ¯Xn ≥ϑ

Pp1

The infimum defining V (uk,vk) in (EC.34) is attained. Indeed, f(ϑ) := max{uk/D0(ϑ),vk/D1(ϑ)}
is continuous on (p0,p1); as ϑ ↓p0 we have D0(ϑ) ↓0, and as ϑ ↑p1 we have D1(ϑ) ↓0, so f(ϑ) →+∞
at both endpoints. Extending f by +∞to the endpoints makes it lower semicontinuous and coercive
on the compact interval [p0,p1]; it therefore attains its minimum, necessarily at an interior point
ϑ⋆∈(p0,p1), and that minimum value is V (uk,vk) by (EC.34).
Define nk := ⌈V (uk,vk)⌉. Then nk ≥V (uk,vk) ≥uk/D0(ϑ⋆) and nk ≥V (uk,vk) ≥vk/D1(ϑ⋆), so
nkD0(ϑ⋆) ≥uk and nkD1(ϑ⋆) ≥vk.
By the definition of Nfixed,H (the smallest sample size at which some test on i.i.d. Bernoulli labels
achieves the target errors), to prove Nfixed,H(αk,βk) ≤nk it suffices to exhibit one feasible test on
nk labels. We use the sample-mean test that rejects H0 when ¯Xnk ≥ϑ⋆. By (EC.39),
Pp0( ¯Xnk ≥ϑ⋆) ≤e−nkD0(ϑ⋆) ≤e−uk = αk,
Pp1( ¯Xnk ≤ϑ⋆) ≤e−nkD1(ϑ⋆) ≤e−vk = βk.
This test is feasible at (αk,βk), so
Nfixed,H(αk,βk) ≤nk = ⌈V (uk,vk)⌉≤V (uk,vk) + 1.

(EC.45)

Finding a good ϑkℓ∈(p0,p1). We claim that (EC.46) implies the existence of ϑkℓ∈(p0,p1) with
nkℓD0(ϑkℓ) < ukℓ−δkℓ,
nkℓD1(ϑkℓ) < vkℓ−δkℓ.
(EC.47)
The strategy is to define two open subsets of (p0,p1), one where the first inequality of (EC.47)
holds and one where the second holds, show that they cover (p0,p1), and use connectedness to
conclude they must overlap.
By the definition of V as an infimum, nkℓ< V (ukℓ−δkℓ,vkℓ−δkℓ) means: for every ϑ ∈(p0,p1),

e-companion
This is equivalent to: for every ϑ ∈(p0,p1), D0(ϑ) < (ukℓ−δkℓ)/nkℓor D1(ϑ) < (vkℓ−δkℓ)/nkℓ.
Define the two sets
U :=

ϑ ∈(p0,p1) : D0(ϑ) < ukℓ−δkℓ
nkℓ

,
V ′ :=

ϑ ∈(p0,p1) : D1(ϑ) < vkℓ−δkℓ
nkℓ

.
The displayed equivalence above says exactly U ∪V ′ = (p0,p1). Any point ϑkℓ∈U ∩V ′ satisfies
both inequalities in (EC.47); we now show U ∩V ′̸ = ∅.
The set U is open in (p0,p1) because D0 is continuous; it contains a right-neighborhood of
p0 because D0(p0) = 0 < (ukℓ−δkℓ)/nkℓand D0 is continuous at p0, so D0(ϑ) < (ukℓ−δkℓ)/nkℓ
for ϑ sufficiently close to p0. In particular, U is nonempty. Similarly, V ′ is open and contains a
left-neighborhood of p1 (since D1(p1) = 0), so V ′ is nonempty.
If U ∩V ′ = ∅, then U and V ′ would be two disjoint nonempty open subsets of (p0,p1) whose
union is (p0,p1). This contradicts connectedness of the interval (p0,p1). Hence U ∩V ′̸ = ∅; any
ϑkℓ∈U ∩V ′ satisfies (EC.47).
Discretizing. The point-mass lower bound (EC.40) is stated for binomial point masses at integer

Proof of Lemma EC.8
Fix a sufficiently large k. We prove the bound for W1,k under H1; the

EC.3.6.
Proof of Lemma EC.11
Proof of Lemma EC.11
Fix a sufficiently large k. The human fallback completion cost is at
most cHNfixed,H(α2,k,β2,k) = O(Lk) from Lemma EC.13. If the AI fallback is unavailable, it is never
selected. If it is selected, then Nfixed,AI(α2,k,β2,k) ≤Nmain,k = O(Lk) by the definition of availability
in the pre-commitment rule, so its completion cost is also O(Lk). Hence the pre-committed fallback
cost is deterministically at most CLk for a primitive constant C, on both the human and AI
branches. Since Cfb

taking Eπk

Eπk

h (Efb),
and substituting the probability estimate of Lemma EC.10 proves (EC.29) with CF := C Cfb.

frequencies Mx(r)/Mx, a deterministic bound on | ˆfx(r) −Mx(r)/Mx|, and a combination of the
two that also yields the rate.
